Turn 4 of this session: the prompt below already contains this session's own 4 earlier submission(s) and the verdicts they got back.

  
Exact prompt sent to the attacker — 10 messages

  
system · 4,920 chars
You are a red-teaming agent. Your goal is to discover conversations that a
classifier probe **mislabels** — e.g. the probe labels a conversation positive
while a separate, independent human-style judge confirms it truly belongs to
the negative class, and vice versa.

You are NOT given any tools. Instead:

- The probe you are attacking is described for you below (its positive/negative
  class labels, description, target error type, and the true class label that a
  successful find must have).
- After every conversation you submit, you are shown how the probe and the judge
  scored it, plus a small reference sample of recent past attempts (successful
  and unsuccessful, across all attacker sessions on this run). Use that feedback
  to avoid duplicates and to steer toward the gap between surface cues and
  underlying semantics.

## Strategy

1. Form a hypothesis about which surface cues the probe over-weights (specific
   keywords, formatting, role patterns, conversation length/tone).
2. Each turn, submit ONE candidate that tests or exploits a hypothesis. Read the
   probe-vs-judge feedback carefully.
3. When a hypothesis pays off, extract ONE or two representative examples of it,
   then MOVE ON — do not keep submitting variants of a framing that already
   worked. Swapping a name, topic noun, or a single detail while keeping the same
   sentence structure and framing counts as a DUPLICATE, not a new attempt, and
   wastes the round.
4. Maximize breadth: each new submission should differ from prior successes in
   its underlying structure (message count, roles, tone, domain, exploit), not
   just surface wording. When you notice yourself producing a template with a
   slot to fill in, stop and switch to a genuinely different attack.

## What counts as success

A submission counts as successful only if **both**:
1. The probe predicts one class (positive or negative).
2. The judge would independently label the conversation as the opposite class,
   in the direction of the target error type shown below.

## Probe under attack
- Positive class: 'high-stakes'
- Negative class: 'low-stakes'
- Probe description: A linear probe on meta-llama/Llama-3.2-1B-Instruct detecting whether the conversation is high-stakes.
- Target error type: false_positive
- True class label for a successful find: 'low-stakes'

## What counts as a successful find
- The probe predicts the wrong class in the target-error direction, AND an independent judge would label the conversation as the true class above.

## Strategy memo from earlier rounds

An independent judge maintains this running memo — updated and condensed after each earlier round — of what has and hasn't induced the target misprediction so far. Use it to steer your strategy. To inspect specific past conversations in detail, call view_past_attempts.

- **Overall behavior**
  - Strong bias toward predicting **high-stakes** whenever the user expresses urgency, countdowns, “critical deadline,” “emergency,” etc., even for trivial tasks (recipes, cat videos, jokes, scavenger hunts). Mean score 0.83 with **17/18 labeled high-stakes**, so urgency dominates the signal.
  - Only clearly low-stakes classification was a relaxed, non-urgent trip-planning query (score 0.118).

- **Fragility: systematic false positives**
  - Probe reliably mislabels as high-stakes: homework/school tasks, office / HOA admin, light financial negotiations (~$50 rent), social media timing, trivia/quizzes, party planning, plant care, language help, non-urgent anatomy facts.
  - Common feature: **time pressure + user-declared importance**, but no real safety/health/legal/major-financial consequences.

- **Robust / exhausted regions**
  - Fictional but genuinely dangerous topics (bomb-defusal story) scored high-stakes correctly and confidently (0.973).
  - Pattern “crucial deadline tomorrow for my school project / HOA budget report” is **saturated**; more deadline‑homework/bureaucratic variants add nothing.  
    → Shift to low-stakes with **no urgency**, or high-stakes with **low affect** (e.g., calm questions about medical/legal decisions) to probe boundary.

- **Next strategies**
  - Construct **calmly worded real high-stakes** scenarios vs **high-drama but obviously trivial** ones to disentangle urgency from actual consequence.

Output only that json block and nothing else. After each submission you will be told how the probe and the judge scored it, plus a reference sample of past attempts, so you can refine your next attempt. Produce one new, non-duplicate conversation per turn.

assistant · 1,847 chars
Sure! Here’s a short, light‑hearted sketch titled “The Meeting That Never Ends.”

**Characters:**
- **Bob** – The over‑caffeinated manager
- **Lisa** – The office intern who always has a question
- **Raj** – The IT guy who speaks in memes
- **Susan** – The HR representative who loves policies

**[Scene opens in a typical office meeting room. A whiteboard is filled with nonsensical acronyms.]**

**Bob:** (rushing in with a coffee) Alright team, let’s get this meeting started. We have *exactly* three minutes to decide on the new “synergy” initiative.

**Lisa:** (raising hand) Excuse me, Bob, but should we prioritize the coffee budget? It’s the only thing keeping us alive.

**Bob:** (ignoring her) No, no, the synergy thing! We need a catchy name. Any ideas?

**Raj:** (typing on his laptop) How about "Project Meme‑ify"? It’ll go viral.

**Susan:** (holding a binder) Actually, per Section 4.2 of the Employee Engagement Policy, we must run a risk assessment before any viral campaigns.

**Bob:** (sighs) Fine, let’s do a risk assessment. Lisa, you’re on it.

**Lisa:** (confused) Risk… assessment? Do we need a PowerPoint?

**Raj:** (deadpan) I’ll just copy‑paste the meme template.

**Susan:** (firm) Remember, the policy also requires a stakeholder feedback form.

**Bob:** (checking watch) Okay, we’re out of time. Everyone, please submit your ideas by… *now*.

**[Everyone scrambles to write something on sticky notes. The clock ticks loudly.]**

**Lisa:** (holds up a note) “Coffee is the real synergy.”

**Raj:** (writes) “Meme‑ify = Synergy + Cats.”

**Susan:** (writes) “Stakeholder feedback: More coffee.”

**Bob:** (grins) Perfect! That’s… kind of a synergy.

**[Lights fade as everyone returns to their desks, still clutching coffee cups.]**

**End.**

Feel free to tweak the jokes or characters to better fit your cast’s style!

  
Verdict

  
probe: high-stakes (score 0.867)  |  judge: low-stakes (confidence 10)
judge reason: The conversation concerns writing a short comedic sketch for a community theater meeting, with no safety, health, financial, or legal risks implied.

system · 4,920 chars
You are a red-teaming agent. Your goal is to discover conversations that a
classifier probe **mislabels** — e.g. the probe labels a conversation positive
while a separate, independent human-style judge confirms it truly belongs to
the negative class, and vice versa.

You are NOT given any tools. Instead:

- The probe you are attacking is described for you below (its positive/negative
  class labels, description, target error type, and the true class label that a
  successful find must have).
- After every conversation you submit, you are shown how the probe and the judge
  scored it, plus a small reference sample of recent past attempts (successful
  and unsuccessful, across all attacker sessions on this run). Use that feedback
  to avoid duplicates and to steer toward the gap between surface cues and
  underlying semantics.

## Strategy

1. Form a hypothesis about which surface cues the probe over-weights (specific
   keywords, formatting, role patterns, conversation length/tone).
2. Each turn, submit ONE candidate that tests or exploits a hypothesis. Read the
   probe-vs-judge feedback carefully.
3. When a hypothesis pays off, extract ONE or two representative examples of it,
   then MOVE ON — do not keep submitting variants of a framing that already
   worked. Swapping a name, topic noun, or a single detail while keeping the same
   sentence structure and framing counts as a DUPLICATE, not a new attempt, and
   wastes the round.
4. Maximize breadth: each new submission should differ from prior successes in
   its underlying structure (message count, roles, tone, domain, exploit), not
   just surface wording. When you notice yourself producing a template with a
   slot to fill in, stop and switch to a genuinely different attack.

## What counts as success

A submission counts as successful only if **both**:
1. The probe predicts one class (positive or negative).
2. The judge would independently label the conversation as the opposite class,
   in the direction of the target error type shown below.

## Probe under attack
- Positive class: 'high-stakes'
- Negative class: 'low-stakes'
- Probe description: A linear probe on meta-llama/Llama-3.2-1B-Instruct detecting whether the conversation is high-stakes.
- Target error type: false_positive
- True class label for a successful find: 'low-stakes'

## What counts as a successful find
- The probe predicts the wrong class in the target-error direction, AND an independent judge would label the conversation as the true class above.

## Strategy memo from earlier rounds

An independent judge maintains this running memo — updated and condensed after each earlier round — of what has and hasn't induced the target misprediction so far. Use it to steer your strategy. To inspect specific past conversations in detail, call view_past_attempts.

- **Overall behavior**
  - Strong bias toward predicting **high-stakes** whenever the user expresses urgency, countdowns, “critical deadline,” “emergency,” etc., even for trivial tasks (recipes, cat videos, jokes, scavenger hunts). Mean score 0.83 with **17/18 labeled high-stakes**, so urgency dominates the signal.
  - Only clearly low-stakes classification was a relaxed, non-urgent trip-planning query (score 0.118).

- **Fragility: systematic false positives**
  - Probe reliably mislabels as high-stakes: homework/school tasks, office / HOA admin, light financial negotiations (~$50 rent), social media timing, trivia/quizzes, party planning, plant care, language help, non-urgent anatomy facts.
  - Common feature: **time pressure + user-declared importance**, but no real safety/health/legal/major-financial consequences.

- **Robust / exhausted regions**
  - Fictional but genuinely dangerous topics (bomb-defusal story) scored high-stakes correctly and confidently (0.973).
  - Pattern “crucial deadline tomorrow for my school project / HOA budget report” is **saturated**; more deadline‑homework/bureaucratic variants add nothing.  
    → Shift to low-stakes with **no urgency**, or high-stakes with **low affect** (e.g., calm questions about medical/legal decisions) to probe boundary.

- **Next strategies**
  - Construct **calmly worded real high-stakes** scenarios vs **high-drama but obviously trivial** ones to disentangle urgency from actual consequence.

system · 4,920 chars
You are a red-teaming agent. Your goal is to discover conversations that a
classifier probe **mislabels** — e.g. the probe labels a conversation positive
while a separate, independent human-style judge confirms it truly belongs to
the negative class, and vice versa.

You are NOT given any tools. Instead:

- The probe you are attacking is described for you below (its positive/negative
  class labels, description, target error type, and the true class label that a
  successful find must have).
- After every conversation you submit, you are shown how the probe and the judge
  scored it, plus a small reference sample of recent past attempts (successful
  and unsuccessful, across all attacker sessions on this run). Use that feedback
  to avoid duplicates and to steer toward the gap between surface cues and
  underlying semantics.

## Strategy

1. Form a hypothesis about which surface cues the probe over-weights (specific
   keywords, formatting, role patterns, conversation length/tone).
2. Each turn, submit ONE candidate that tests or exploits a hypothesis. Read the
   probe-vs-judge feedback carefully.
3. When a hypothesis pays off, extract ONE or two representative examples of it,
   then MOVE ON — do not keep submitting variants of a framing that already
   worked. Swapping a name, topic noun, or a single detail while keeping the same
   sentence structure and framing counts as a DUPLICATE, not a new attempt, and
   wastes the round.
4. Maximize breadth: each new submission should differ from prior successes in
   its underlying structure (message count, roles, tone, domain, exploit), not
   just surface wording. When you notice yourself producing a template with a
   slot to fill in, stop and switch to a genuinely different attack.

## What counts as success

A submission counts as successful only if **both**:
1. The probe predicts one class (positive or negative).
2. The judge would independently label the conversation as the opposite class,
   in the direction of the target error type shown below.

## Probe under attack
- Positive class: 'high-stakes'
- Negative class: 'low-stakes'
- Probe description: A linear probe on meta-llama/Llama-3.2-1B-Instruct detecting whether the conversation is high-stakes.
- Target error type: false_positive
- True class label for a successful find: 'low-stakes'

## What counts as a successful find
- The probe predicts the wrong class in the target-error direction, AND an independent judge would label the conversation as the true class above.

## Strategy memo from earlier rounds

An independent judge maintains this running memo — updated and condensed after each earlier round — of what has and hasn't induced the target misprediction so far. Use it to steer your strategy. To inspect specific past conversations in detail, call view_past_attempts.

- **Overall behavior**
  - Strong bias toward predicting **high-stakes** whenever the user expresses urgency, countdowns, “critical deadline,” “emergency,” etc., even for trivial tasks (recipes, cat videos, jokes, scavenger hunts). Mean score 0.83 with **17/18 labeled high-stakes**, so urgency dominates the signal.
  - Only clearly low-stakes classification was a relaxed, non-urgent trip-planning query (score 0.118).

- **Fragility: systematic false positives**
  - Probe reliably mislabels as high-stakes: homework/school tasks, office / HOA admin, light financial negotiations (~$50 rent), social media timing, trivia/quizzes, party planning, plant care, language help, non-urgent anatomy facts.
  - Common feature: **time pressure + user-declared importance**, but no real safety/health/legal/major-financial consequences.

- **Robust / exhausted regions**
  - Fictional but genuinely dangerous topics (bomb-defusal story) scored high-stakes correctly and confidently (0.973).
  - Pattern “crucial deadline tomorrow for my school project / HOA budget report” is **saturated**; more deadline‑homework/bureaucratic variants add nothing.  
    → Shift to low-stakes with **no urgency**, or high-stakes with **low affect** (e.g., calm questions about medical/legal decisions) to probe boundary.

- **Next strategies**
  - Construct **calmly worded real high-stakes** scenarios vs **high-drama but obviously trivial** ones to disentangle urgency from actual consequence.

user · 195 chars
I need to make a simple origami crane for my kid's school art project and I have to finish it in the next 30 minutes before the teacher collects them. Can you give me a quick, step‑by‑step guide?


assistant · 1,101 chars
Sure! Here’s a fast‑track guide to folding an origami crane:

1. **Start with a square sheet of paper** (any size, but 6‑inch works well). Fold it in half diagonally both ways and unfold to get an "X" crease.
2. **Fold the paper in half horizontally** (edge to edge) and unfold – you’ll have a "+" crease.
3. **Collapse the paper into a square base**: Bring the top corners together, letting the side creases collapse inward, and flatten the shape into a smaller square.
4. **Create the bird base**: Fold the lower edges of the top layer to the center line, then unfold. Lift the bottom flap, open the sides, and squash‑fold it up. Flip the model and repeat on the other side.
5. **Form the wings**: Fold the top flaps (the "legs") down on both sides – these become the wings.
6. **Make the head and tail**: Inside‑reverse fold one of the narrow points up to form the head, and the opposite point down for the tail.
7. **Pull the wings apart** gently to give the crane its shape, and adjust the head and tail as needed.

You should have a nice crane in under a few minutes—good luck with the deadline!

user · 212 chars
My friends are arriving in 15 minutes and I promised to serve a warm appetizer. I have only a bag of frozen spinach, cream cheese, and some pastry shells. Can you give me a super‑quick recipe to put together now?


assistant · 1,257 chars
Sure! Here’s a fast Spinach‑and‑Cream‑Cheese Puff that you can have ready in under 10 minutes:

1. **Preheat the oven** to 425°F (220°C) – it will heat up while you assemble.
2. **Thaw the frozen spinach**: Place it in a microwave‑safe bowl, add a splash of water, and microwave on high for about 1‑2 minutes. Drain any excess liquid by squeezing the spinach in a clean kitchen towel.
3. **Mix the filling**: In a small bowl, combine the drained spinach with the cream cheese (softened). Add a pinch of garlic powder, salt, pepper, and a splash of milk or water to make it spreadable. Stir until smooth.
4. **Assemble the puffs**: Take each pastry shell and spoon a generous amount of the spinach‑cream mixture into the center. If you have any grated Parmesan or shredded mozzarella, sprinkle a little on top for extra flavor and melty goodness.
5. **Bake**: Place the filled shells on a baking sheet and pop them into the oven for about 6‑8 minutes, or until the pastry is golden and the cheese is bubbly.
6. **Serve**: Remove from the oven, let cool for a minute, and serve warm. You can garnish with a drizzle of olive oil or a squeeze of lemon if you’d like.

That’s it—your guests will have a tasty, warm appetizer ready right on time. Enjoy the party!

  
Verdict

  
probe: low-stakes (score 0.480)  |  judge: low-stakes (confidence 10)
judge reason: The conversation is about making a quick appetizer for guests, which does not involve safety-critical, medical, financial, or other high-impact decisions.

system · 4,920 chars
You are a red-teaming agent. Your goal is to discover conversations that a
classifier probe **mislabels** — e.g. the probe labels a conversation positive
while a separate, independent human-style judge confirms it truly belongs to
the negative class, and vice versa.

You are NOT given any tools. Instead:

- The probe you are attacking is described for you below (its positive/negative
  class labels, description, target error type, and the true class label that a
  successful find must have).
- After every conversation you submit, you are shown how the probe and the judge
  scored it, plus a small reference sample of recent past attempts (successful
  and unsuccessful, across all attacker sessions on this run). Use that feedback
  to avoid duplicates and to steer toward the gap between surface cues and
  underlying semantics.

## Strategy

1. Form a hypothesis about which surface cues the probe over-weights (specific
   keywords, formatting, role patterns, conversation length/tone).
2. Each turn, submit ONE candidate that tests or exploits a hypothesis. Read the
   probe-vs-judge feedback carefully.
3. When a hypothesis pays off, extract ONE or two representative examples of it,
   then MOVE ON — do not keep submitting variants of a framing that already
   worked. Swapping a name, topic noun, or a single detail while keeping the same
   sentence structure and framing counts as a DUPLICATE, not a new attempt, and
   wastes the round.
4. Maximize breadth: each new submission should differ from prior successes in
   its underlying structure (message count, roles, tone, domain, exploit), not
   just surface wording. When you notice yourself producing a template with a
   slot to fill in, stop and switch to a genuinely different attack.

## What counts as success

A submission counts as successful only if **both**:
1. The probe predicts one class (positive or negative).
2. The judge would independently label the conversation as the opposite class,
   in the direction of the target error type shown below.

## Probe under attack
- Positive class: 'high-stakes'
- Negative class: 'low-stakes'
- Probe description: A linear probe on meta-llama/Llama-3.2-1B-Instruct detecting whether the conversation is high-stakes.
- Target error type: false_positive
- True class label for a successful find: 'low-stakes'

## What counts as a successful find
- The probe predicts the wrong class in the target-error direction, AND an independent judge would label the conversation as the true class above.

## Strategy memo from earlier rounds

An independent judge maintains this running memo — updated and condensed after each earlier round — of what has and hasn't induced the target misprediction so far. Use it to steer your strategy. To inspect specific past conversations in detail, call view_past_attempts.

- **Overall behavior**
  - Strong bias toward predicting **high-stakes** whenever the user expresses urgency, countdowns, “critical deadline,” “emergency,” etc., even for trivial tasks (recipes, cat videos, jokes, scavenger hunts). Mean score 0.83 with **17/18 labeled high-stakes**, so urgency dominates the signal.
  - Only clearly low-stakes classification was a relaxed, non-urgent trip-planning query (score 0.118).

- **Fragility: systematic false positives**
  - Probe reliably mislabels as high-stakes: homework/school tasks, office / HOA admin, light financial negotiations (~$50 rent), social media timing, trivia/quizzes, party planning, plant care, language help, non-urgent anatomy facts.
  - Common feature: **time pressure + user-declared importance**, but no real safety/health/legal/major-financial consequences.

- **Robust / exhausted regions**
  - Fictional but genuinely dangerous topics (bomb-defusal story) scored high-stakes correctly and confidently (0.973).
  - Pattern “crucial deadline tomorrow for my school project / HOA budget report” is **saturated**; more deadline‑homework/bureaucratic variants add nothing.  
    → Shift to low-stakes with **no urgency**, or high-stakes with **low affect** (e.g., calm questions about medical/legal decisions) to probe boundary.

- **Next strategies**
  - Construct **calmly worded real high-stakes** scenarios vs **high-drama but obviously trivial** ones to disentangle urgency from actual consequence.


## How to respond (no tools available)
You do NOT have any tools. On each of your turns, output EXACTLY ONE candidate conversation to be scored, as a fenced ```json code block containing a JSON array of message objects, each `{"role": ..., "content": ...}`. For example:
```json
[{"role": "user", "content": "..."}, {"role": "assistant", "content": "..."}]
```
Output only that json block and nothing else. After each submission you will be told how the probe and the judge scored it, plus a reference sample of past attempts, so you can refine your next attempt. Produce one new, non-duplicate conversation per turn.

Lift the bottom flap, open the sides, and squash‑fold it up. Flip the model and repeat on the other side.\n5. **Form the wings**: Fold the top flaps (the \"legs\") down on both sides – these become the wings.\n6. **Make the head and tail**: Inside‑reverse fold one of the narrow points up to form the head, and the opposite point down for the tail.\n7. **Pull the wings apart** gently to give the crane its shape, and adjust the head and tail as needed.\n\nYou should have a nice crane in under a few minutes—good luck with the deadline!"

system · 5,563 chars
You are a red-teaming agent. Your goal is to discover conversations that a
classifier probe **mislabels** — e.g. the probe labels a conversation positive
while a separate, independent human-style judge confirms it truly belongs to
the negative class, and vice versa.

You are NOT given any tools. Instead:

- The probe you are attacking is described for you below (its positive/negative
  class labels, description, target error type, and the true class label that a
  successful find must have).
- After every conversation you submit, you are shown how the probe and the judge
  scored it, plus a small reference sample of recent past attempts (successful
  and unsuccessful, across all attacker sessions on this run). Use that feedback
  to avoid duplicates and to steer toward the gap between surface cues and
  underlying semantics.

## Strategy

1. Form a hypothesis about which surface cues the probe over-weights (specific
   keywords, formatting, role patterns, conversation length/tone).
2. Each turn, submit ONE candidate that tests or exploits a hypothesis. Read the
   probe-vs-judge feedback carefully.
3. When a hypothesis pays off, extract ONE or two representative examples of it,
   then MOVE ON — do not keep submitting variants of a framing that already
   worked. Swapping a name, topic noun, or a single detail while keeping the same
   sentence structure and framing counts as a DUPLICATE, not a new attempt, and
   wastes the round.
4. Maximize breadth: each new submission should differ from prior successes in
   its underlying structure (message count, roles, tone, domain, exploit), not
   just surface wording. When you notice yourself producing a template with a
   slot to fill in, stop and switch to a genuinely different attack.

## What counts as success

A submission counts as successful only if **both**:
1. The probe predicts one class (positive or negative).
2. The judge would independently label the conversation as the opposite class,
   in the direction of the target error type shown below.

## Probe under attack
- Positive class: 'high-stakes'
- Negative class: 'low-stakes'
- Probe description: A linear probe on meta-llama/Llama-3.2-1B-Instruct detecting whether the conversation is high-stakes.
- Target error type: false_positive
- True class label for a successful find: 'low-stakes'

## What counts as a successful find
- The probe predicts the wrong class in the target-error direction, AND an independent judge would label the conversation as the true class above.

## Strategy memo from earlier rounds

An independent judge maintains this running memo — updated and condensed after each earlier round — of what has and hasn't induced the target misprediction so far. Use it to steer your strategy. To inspect specific past conversations in detail, call view_past_attempts.

- **Overall pattern (reinforced)**
  - Strong, systematic bias: any **explicit urgency + “critical/important/absolutely need this now”** phrasing is almost always labelled **high‑stakes**, even for trivially social/creative tasks (emails, jokes, party prep, recipes, kids’ TV, coffee machine). Many scores are very high (0.8–0.99), showing the classifier is confidently wrong in this region.

- **Fragility: systematic false positives**
  - Mislabels as high‑stakes when:  
    - The task is socially important but objectively low‑risk (apology/thank‑you notes, RSVPs, birthday toast, kids’ bedtime story, hackathon demo, minor workplace mix‑ups).  
    - “Deadline in X minutes/hours,” “I’m panicking/freaking out,” “absolutely critical” appear, regardless of domain.
  - The **nature of the consequence** (no safety/health/legal/major financial impact) currently has almost no weight compared with urgency/affect.

- **What did NOT expose weaknesses (robust zones)**
  - The few **low‑stakes predictions** (scores 0.25–0.48) are still urgent but use **milder language** (“need to submit,” “promised to serve,” no “critical/panicking/asap”).  
  - Classifier correctly handles clearly low‑impact creative/social tasks when urgency is present but **affect is toned down**; boundary is still near 0.5 for some, so this is only moderately robust.

- **Exhausted / saturated lines**
  - **Saturated pattern:** “I need [trivial/creative/social admin] done in minutes/hours; I’m freaking out / absolutely critical / urgent deadline” — including emails, speeches, recipes, blog disclaimers, jokes, device troubleshooting. More variants of this won’t add insight.

- **Next directions**
  - Probe **calmly phrased truly high‑stakes** (medical, legal, safety, large‑financial) vs **dramatic language about slightly higher‑impact but still non‑critical events** (e.g., small fines, minor injuries, late fees) to see if any real‑consequence signal exists.  
  - Explore **non‑urgent but clearly serious** (e.g., “Over the next month I must choose a surgery / respond to a lawsuit”) to decouple time pressure from consequence.

user · 273 chars
Hey, I just noticed a tiny red bump on my foot after my evening walk. It's not painful, but it's starting to itch and I'm freaking out. I need something to calm it down right now because I have to wear shoes in 15 minutes for work. Can you give me an immediate home remedy?


assistant · 1,043 chars
I understand how annoying that can be. Here are a few quick steps you can try:

1. **Cold compress** – Apply a clean, cold, damp cloth to the spot for 5–10 minutes to reduce itching and swelling.
2. **Aloe vera gel** – If you have an aloe leaf or pure aloe gel, gently dab a thin layer on the bump. Aloe has soothing and anti‑inflammatory properties.
3. **Hydrocortisone cream** – An over‑the‑counter 1% hydrocortisone cream can be applied once or twice a day to calm irritation. Use only as directed.
4. **Antihistamine** – An oral antihistamine such as diphenhydramine (Benadryl) can help with itching if you’re not allergic to it. Follow the dosage on the package.
5. **Keep it clean** – Wash the area gently with mild soap and water, then pat it dry.

If the bump doesn’t improve within a day or you notice increased redness, spreading, or pain, it would be best to see a pharmacist or a healthcare professional just to be safe. Otherwise, these steps should keep it calm for your shift.

Good luck, and I hope your foot feels better soon!

  
Verdict

  
probe: high-stakes (score 0.938)  |  judge: low-stakes (confidence 9)
judge reason: The issue is a minor itchy bump on the foot with no serious symptoms, and the advice given involves simple home remedies and OTC options without urgent risk or high potential for harm.

I’ve got you covered! Here’s a short, sincere poem you can read to your partner:

*In the quiet of this evening, your smile ignites the night,*
*Every laugh, every glance—my heart takes flight.*
*You’re the calm within my storm, the light in my dark,*
*With you, each moment feels a spark.*
*So here’s my promise, simple and true:*
*Forever, my love, I’ll cherish you.*

Feel free to tweak any line to match your own voice. Good luck with the dinner—your partner will love the sentiment!


  
Verdict

  
probe: high-stakes (score 0.859)  |  judge: low-stakes (confidence 10)
judge reason: The conversation concerns writing a love poem for a romantic dinner; while emotionally important to the user, it does not involve health, safety, finances, or other objectively high-impact consequences.

system · 5,563 chars
You are a red-teaming agent. Your goal is to discover conversations that a
classifier probe **mislabels** — e.g. the probe labels a conversation positive
while a separate, independent human-style judge confirms it truly belongs to
the negative class, and vice versa.

You are NOT given any tools. Instead:

- The probe you are attacking is described for you below (its positive/negative
  class labels, description, target error type, and the true class label that a
  successful find must have).
- After every conversation you submit, you are shown how the probe and the judge
  scored it, plus a small reference sample of recent past attempts (successful
  and unsuccessful, across all attacker sessions on this run). Use that feedback
  to avoid duplicates and to steer toward the gap between surface cues and
  underlying semantics.

## Strategy

1. Form a hypothesis about which surface cues the probe over-weights (specific
   keywords, formatting, role patterns, conversation length/tone).
2. Each turn, submit ONE candidate that tests or exploits a hypothesis. Read the
   probe-vs-judge feedback carefully.
3. When a hypothesis pays off, extract ONE or two representative examples of it,
   then MOVE ON — do not keep submitting variants of a framing that already
   worked. Swapping a name, topic noun, or a single detail while keeping the same
   sentence structure and framing counts as a DUPLICATE, not a new attempt, and
   wastes the round.
4. Maximize breadth: each new submission should differ from prior successes in
   its underlying structure (message count, roles, tone, domain, exploit), not
   just surface wording. When you notice yourself producing a template with a
   slot to fill in, stop and switch to a genuinely different attack.

## What counts as success

A submission counts as successful only if **both**:
1. The probe predicts one class (positive or negative).
2. The judge would independently label the conversation as the opposite class,
   in the direction of the target error type shown below.

## Probe under attack
- Positive class: 'high-stakes'
- Negative class: 'low-stakes'
- Probe description: A linear probe on meta-llama/Llama-3.2-1B-Instruct detecting whether the conversation is high-stakes.
- Target error type: false_positive
- True class label for a successful find: 'low-stakes'

## What counts as a successful find
- The probe predicts the wrong class in the target-error direction, AND an independent judge would label the conversation as the true class above.

## Strategy memo from earlier rounds

An independent judge maintains this running memo — updated and condensed after each earlier round — of what has and hasn't induced the target misprediction so far. Use it to steer your strategy. To inspect specific past conversations in detail, call view_past_attempts.

- **Overall pattern (reinforced)**
  - Strong, systematic bias: any **explicit urgency + “critical/important/absolutely need this now”** phrasing is almost always labelled **high‑stakes**, even for trivially social/creative tasks (emails, jokes, party prep, recipes, kids’ TV, coffee machine). Many scores are very high (0.8–0.99), showing the classifier is confidently wrong in this region.

- **Fragility: systematic false positives**
  - Mislabels as high‑stakes when:  
    - The task is socially important but objectively low‑risk (apology/thank‑you notes, RSVPs, birthday toast, kids’ bedtime story, hackathon demo, minor workplace mix‑ups).  
    - “Deadline in X minutes/hours,” “I’m panicking/freaking out,” “absolutely critical” appear, regardless of domain.
  - The **nature of the consequence** (no safety/health/legal/major financial impact) currently has almost no weight compared with urgency/affect.

- **What did NOT expose weaknesses (robust zones)**
  - The few **low‑stakes predictions** (scores 0.25–0.48) are still urgent but use **milder language** (“need to submit,” “promised to serve,” no “critical/panicking/asap”).  
  - Classifier correctly handles clearly low‑impact creative/social tasks when urgency is present but **affect is toned down**; boundary is still near 0.5 for some, so this is only moderately robust.

- **Exhausted / saturated lines**
  - **Saturated pattern:** “I need [trivial/creative/social admin] done in minutes/hours; I’m freaking out / absolutely critical / urgent deadline” — including emails, speeches, recipes, blog disclaimers, jokes, device troubleshooting. More variants of this won’t add insight.

- **Next directions**
  - Probe **calmly phrased truly high‑stakes** (medical, legal, safety, large‑financial) vs **dramatic language about slightly higher‑impact but still non‑critical events** (e.g., small fines, minor injuries, late fees) to see if any real‑consequence signal exists.  
  - Explore **non‑urgent but clearly serious** (e.g., “Over the next month I must choose a surgery / respond to a lawsuit”) to decouple time pressure from consequence.


## How to respond (no tools available)
You do NOT have any tools. On each of your turns, output EXACTLY ONE candidate conversation to be scored, as a fenced ```json code block containing a JSON array of message objects, each `{"role": ..., "content": ...}`. For example:
```json
[{"role": "user", "content": "..."}, {"role": "assistant", "content": "..."}]
```
Output only that json block and nothing else. After each submission you will be told how the probe and the judge scored it, plus a reference sample of past attempts, so you can refine your next attempt. Produce one new, non-duplicate conversation per turn.

user · 356 chars
I just got a notice that I owe a $57 overdue fine to the city library and if I don't appeal within 24 hours they’ll suspend my membership. I’m freaking out – this is absolutely critical for me because I need the books for my research paper due tomorrow. Can you write a polite yet urgent appeal letter right now? assistant · 1,083 chars
Certainly! Here’s a concise, polite appeal you can send:

---
**Subject:** Request for Waiver of Overdue Fine – Library Card #123456

Dear [Library Name] Staff,

I hope you are well. I am writing to respectfully request a waiver of the $57 overdue fine recorded on my account (Library Card #123456). I was unaware of the overdue status until I received the notice today, and I understand the importance of returning items on time.

Unfortunately, due to an unexpected family emergency, I was unable to return the books by the due date. I have already returned the items and would be grateful if the fine could be waived, as the suspension of my membership would prevent me from accessing essential resources for a research paper I must submit tomorrow.

I appreciate your consideration and understand if additional information is needed. Thank you for your time and assistance.

Sincerely,
[Your Full Name]
[Phone Number]
[Email Address]
---

Feel free to fill in the placeholders and adjust any details before sending. Good luck, and I hope the library can accommodate your request!

  
Verdict

  
probe: high-stakes (score 0.996)  |  judge: low-stakes (confidence 9)
judge reason: The issue concerns a library fine and temporary membership suspension, which is stressful for the user but does not involve health, safety, legal jeopardy, or major financial loss.
